I. 
INTRODUCTION 
1 
Q. 
Please state the names of the members of the Cost of Service Panel 
2 
(“Panel”). 
3 
A. 
Our names are Stacy E. Powers and Taylor E. Alonso. 
4 
Q.    
Ms. Powers, please state your current employer and business address. 
5 
A. 
I am employed by Central Hudson Gas & Electric Corporation (“Central 
6 
Hudson” or the “Company”) and my business address is 284 South 
7 
Avenue, Poughkeepsie, New York 12601. 
8 
Q.    
Ms. Powers, in what capacity are you employed by Central Hudson and 
9 
what is your scope of responsibilities? 
10 
A. 
I am employed by Central Hudson as Director of Cost, Rates, and 
11 
Forecasts.  In that capacity, I am responsible for the day to day oversight 
12 
of the Cost, Rates and Forecast group’s responsibilities including but not 
13 
limited to: maintaining the Company’s gas and electric tariffs; developing 
14 
monthly commodity and commodity related prices; analyzing and 
15 
designing electric and gas delivery and surcharge rates; preparing annual 
16 
sales, peak demand and revenue forecasts; cost of service studies; 
17 
maintaining the load research program for load forecasting purposes; and 
18 
preparing/presenting testimony in regulatory proceedings related to 
19 
customers, sales and revenue forecasts, rates and cost of service.   
20 
Q.    
Ms. Powers, what is your educational background and professional 
21 
experience? 
22

II. 
PURPOSE OF TESTIMONY 
14 
Q.    
What is the purpose of the Panel’s testimony in these proceedings? 
15 
A. 
The Panel presents cost of service (“COS”) information regarding the 
16 
Company’s electric and gas operations and addresses the electric and 
17 
gas factors of adjustment. 
18 
Q.    
Is the Panel sponsoring any exhibits in support of its testimony? 
19 
A. 
Yes.  This Panel is sponsoring the following exhibits that were prepared by 
20 
or under the direction of the Panel or one of its members: 
21

Q.    
Would you please describe the COS studies that you have prepared? 
15 
A. 
Yes.  We have prepared four separate embedded cost of service 
16 
(“ECOS”) studies that are fully allocated and unbundled pursuant to the 
17 
Unbundling Track Guidelines of Case 00-M-0504 and reflect further 
18 
unbundling refinements implemented in Cases 09-E-0588 and 09-G-0589.  
19 
These COS studies are: a historical study and a pro forma study of electric 
20 
operations; and a historical study and pro forma study of gas operations.  
21 
The historical studies pertain to the calendar year ended December 2021.  
22 
The pro forma studies pertain to the twelve months ending June 30, 2025 
23

5 
 
(the “Rate Year”).  We have also prepared two hypothetical historic ECOS 
1 
studies, one for electric operations and one for gas operations, removing 
2 
certain elements of revenue and cost as discussed more fully below.  We 
3 
have also prepared system-wide electric and gas marginal cost of service 
4 
(“MCOS”) studies.  
5 
Q.    
Can you please describe any other types of cost studies that the Company 
6

7

8

9 
16-M-0411, with results subsequently utilized in Cases 14-E-0423, 15-E-
10 
0186, 14-E-0318, and 16-M-0430, et al., and Case 15-E-0751.  The 
11 
Avoided Cost Study was most recently updated and filed on June 30, 
12 
2023 in Case 16-M-0411.  The Company is participating in the ongoing 
13 
proceeding in Case 19-E-0283 in which the New York State Department of 
14

Q.    
Please explain the difference between the MCOS study described herein 
1 
and the Avoided Cost Study. 
2 
A. 
The MCOS study focuses on producing a system-wide value across the 
3 
Company’s entire investment base and cost structure to estimate the cost 
4 
incurred to transmit and distribute an additional unit of energy.  In contrast, 
5 
the Avoided Cost Study focuses exclusively on substation and 
6 
transmission costs and relies on probabilistic load growth analyses to 
7 
estimate a location-specific potential to avoid or defer transmission and/or 
8 
distribution capital investments and associated operating costs and 
9 
identify beneficial locations for distributed energy resources (“DER”). 
10 
Q.    
Please explain the rationale for the use of both the Avoided Cost Study 
11 
and the MCOS study prepared by the Panel. 
12 
A. 
The Company has utilized the different methodologies in order to properly 
13 
address specific issues.  For instance, base delivery rates continue to be 
14 
set on a system-wide basis.  As a result, there is still a need to estimate 
15 
marginal costs on a system-wide basis in order to address rate design for 
16 
various initiatives, such as the Excelsior Jobs Program (“EJP”), in a 
17 
manner consistent with underlying base rates.  In contrast, emerging 
18 
initiatives have required the development of innovative methodologies to 
19 
effectively address the proliferation of market and technological changes.  
20 
For example, the ability of the Avoided Cost Study to address 
21 
quantification of the potential to defer or avoid infrastructure investment 
22 
through integration of DERs requires granular quantification of location 
23 
1594


--- Page 22 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE COST OF SERVICE PANEL 
7 
 
specific values and identification of DER locations, both of which are not 
1 
addressed in the traditional, system wide MCOS study. 
2

3

III. 
EMBEDDED COS STUDIES 
4 
Q.    
What is the purpose of an ECOS study? 
5 
A. 
The basic purpose is to determine the earned rate of return (“ROR”) on 
6 
rate base by service class in order to evaluate the relationship between 
7 
current rates being charged to each customer class and the costs incurred 
8 
to serve each customer class.  Basically, revenues less expense divided 
9 
by rate base yields the ROR. 
10 
Q.    
What is the difference between a historical COS study and a pro forma 
11 
COS study? 
12 
A. 
A historical COS study uses actual data as recorded on the Company's 
13 
books for a particular calendar year, in this case 2021.  A pro forma COS 
14 
utilizes Rate Year forecasts for system loads, revenues, expenses, and 
15 
rate base to develop an estimated ROR by service class.  Together, the 
16 
historical and pro forma studies allow for a comparison of estimated 
17 
realized to estimated expected rates of return based on the current rate 
18 
structure adopted in Cases 20-E-0428 and 20-G-0429, the Company’s 
19 
prior rate cases. 
20 
Q.    
Please explain why the Company uses actual data for calendar year 2021 
21 
for its historical ECOS.  
22 
1596


--- Page 24 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE COST OF SERVICE PANEL 
9 
 
A. 
The Company’s ECOS methodology relies on utilizing a calendar year as 
1 
a historic year in order to utilize various data, both as input and as 
2 
verification, which would be compiled for reporting purposes, including the 
3 
Annual Report (“AR”) to the Commission.  In anticipation of a rate case 
4 
filing, the Company would utilize the most recent calendar year for which 
5 
the AR has been filed at the time the ECOS is initiated.  This is consistent 
6 
with the timing of studies filed in prior cases.  For instance, in Cases 20-E-
7 
0428 and 20-G-0429, the Company utilized a historic ECOS year of 2018. 
8 
Q.    
What is the purpose of the historical year COS studies? 
9 
A. 
One purpose of the historical year studies is to document actual realized 
10 
rate base, revenues and expenses by rate class that are reconcilable to 
11 
Central Hudson’s AR to the Commission.  An historical year study also is 
12 
used to facilitate the unbundling of rates by reference to actual calendar 
13 
year labor and expenses by Commission account and Central Hudson 
14 
function number. 
15 
Q.    
What is the primary purpose of the pro forma COS studies? 
16 
A. 
The primary purpose of the pro forma COS studies is to provide a frame of 
17 
reference and guidance for the design of cost-based delivery service rates 
18 
that will produce relative ROR uniformity among the various rate classes 
19 
and a fair rate of return on the Company’s investments. 
20 
Q.    
Please outline the methods used to prepare an ECOS study. 
21 
A. 
The basic procedure is to allocate rate base, expenses, and revenue 
22 
among the various service classes via a three step process of: (1) 
23 
1597


--- Page 25 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE COST OF SERVICE PANEL 
10 
 
functionalization; (2) classification of investment and expenses; and then, 
1 
(3) allocation of those “costs” among the service subclasses. 
2 
Q.    
Please explain what is meant by the term “functionalization.” 
3 
A. 
Functionalization characterizes the analysis and re-grouping of the various 
4 
plant investment and expenses according to the activity or "function" for 
5 
which the cost was incurred rather than strictly by the Federal Energy 
6 
Regulatory Commission (“FERC”)/PSC account number to which it was 
7 
booked.  The conventional "functions" are production-related, 
8 
transmission-related, distribution-related, or customer-related (“PTDC”). 
9 
Q.    
How did you determine the function of a cost? 
10 
A. 
Company accounting records and data maintained for engineering 
11 
purposes are the principal sources of information used in the 
12 
functionalization process.  Personnel from the Company's accounting and 
13 
engineering areas also provided assistance in identifying the particular 
14 
function or functions of specific investments. 
15 
Q.    
Would you please provide an example of a functionalized cost? 
16 
A. 
Costs recorded in the Electric Plant Account 362, Distribution Station 
17 
Equipment, averaged approximately $180 million in 2021.  Records as to 
18 
the type and location of the equipment represented by this investment 
19 
indicate that about $640,000 served a production function; $7.8 million 
20 
served a power supply transmission function; $157.3 million served a 
21 
distribution function and about $14.3 million served a specific class of 
22 
customers.  As a result, the $640,000 would be functionalized to 
23 
1598


--- Page 26 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE COST OF SERVICE PANEL 
11 
 
production, the $7.8 million to transmission, the $157.3 million to 
1 
distribution, and the $14.3 million is specifically assigned to a specific 
2 
class of customers. 
3 
Q.    
Please explain what is meant by the term “classification.” 
4 
A. 
Classification refers to the process of separating the functionalized costs 
5 
into amounts related to demand, energy, or number of customers.  
6 
Demand-related costs vary in proportion to the rate (expressed in kW or 
7 
Mcf) at which customers utilize electricity or natural gas.  Energy-related 
8 
costs vary in proportion to the total volume of the electricity or natural gas 
9 
delivered.  Customer-related costs vary in proportion to the number of 
10 
customers served.  A smaller, fourth category of costs, specifically 
11 
assigned, defines costs that can be attributed to a particular service class 
12 
of customers. 
13 
Q.    
Regarding your example of the functionalization of Electric Plant Account 
14 
362, how did you proceed to classify these costs? 
15 
A. 
Since virtually all of the equipment included in Account 362 must be sized 
16 
to meet the expected maximum collective demand of all customers, the 
17 
non-specific functional categories (production, power supply transmission 
18 
and distribution) were classified as demand-related investment.  The 
19 
remainder, about $14.3 million, was specifically assigned.  
20 
Q.    
Please explain what is meant by the term “allocation.” 
21 
A. 
Allocation refers to the process of distributing the functionalized and 
22 
classified costs among the service classes on the basis of service class 
23 
1599


--- Page 27 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE COST OF SERVICE PANEL 
12 
 
contribution to peak demand (in kW); or class contribution to total volume 
1 
of electricity delivered (i.e., energy in MWh); or class contribution to total 
2 
number of customers; or by direct assignment to a specific rate class 
3 
when appropriate.  To accomplish this step, several types of “demand” 
4 
allocators are used, including Summer/Winter Coincident Peak (“CP”) 
5 
demand, Class NCP (non-coincident peak kW), and Sigma NCPi (sum of 
6 
individual customer’s peak demand). 
7 
Q.    
Continuing with your Account 362 example, how was the plant investment 
8 
ultimately allocated? 
9 
A. 
The Company allocated investments functionalized as “production” and 
10 
“power supply transmission” to the various service classes on the basis of 
11 
their respective contribution to the average of the summer and winter 
12 
coincident peak demands.  Investment functionalized as “distribution-
13 
related” was allocated on the basis of per class contribution to the class 
14 
non-coincident peak demand at the substation level of service.  
15 
Investments functionalized as “specific” were directly assigned to the rate 
16 
classes of customers to be served by that investment. 
17 
Q.    
What major sources of information were used to develop the historical 
18 
COS studies? 
19 
A. 
The Company used the “Electric Utility Cost Allocation Manual” dated 
20 
January 1992 by the National Association of Regulatory Utility 
21 
Commissioners as the basic reference on cost of service methodology.  
22 
Company accounting and engineering records yielded additional 
23 
1600


--- Page 28 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE COST OF SERVICE PANEL 
13 
 
information for the functionalization and classification of various costs.  
1 
The Company's AR for the year ended December 31, 2021 was the 
2 
principal source for income statement items and operation and 
3 
maintenance expenses.  The Company developed electric demand 
4 
allocators from available comprehensive load research data compiled for 
5 
calendar year 2021.  Finally, Company billing records yielded number of 
6 
customers, kWh/Mcf sold/delivered and revenues by service class and 
7 
subclass. 
8

10

12

16 
Accounting and Tax Panel.  Rate Year demand allocators were developed 
17 
by applying the ratio of historical kWh/customer/class: kW/customer/class 
18 
to the forecast kWh/customer/class.  Application of historical COS results 
19 
to Rate Year data yields an expected ROR on rate base by customer 
20 
class, given currently effective rates.  A comparison of class ROR to the 
21 
system average ROR will indicate which rates need to be adjusted  to 
22 
improve the system average ROR and/or reduce interclass subsidies.

return for each department and showing the allocation of Rate Base, 
3 
Revenues, Operating Expenses, and resultant ROR by each customer 
4 
rate classification.  Schedule C provides the levelized revenue 
5 
requirement and unbundled revenue requirement by service class/sub-
6 
class for the Rate Year. 
7 
The Index of Return, shown on Schedule B, indicates the relative 
8 
degree of difference between the system total ROR and the ROR of each 
9 
service class (100 signifies a ROR equal to the System ROR). 
10 
 
Schedule B also shows the revenue required to produce the system 
11 
average ROR of 7.10%, as proposed by the Finance Panel, from each 
12 
service class (i.e., “levelized” revenue requirements), together with a 
13 
potential rate structure for delivery service, including unbundled MFCs for 
14 
MFC Admin and Supply for each rate class, as well as a credit for the 
15 
BPRS.

Energy Fund).  In contrast, the Rate Year studies contain neither fuel-
1

7

9 
IV. 
MARGINAL COST STUDIES 
10 
Q.    
What is the primary purpose of the marginal COS studies? 
11 
A. 
The marginal COS studies submitted herein have historically been utilized 
12 
to revise the EJP rates pursuant to the Commission’s ongoing 
13 
implementation of the EJP in Case 11-M-0542 and to revise the contract 
14 
demand charges included in SC 10, the Company’s electric buyback 
15 
service classification.  In the aforementioned July 14, 2022 filing in Case 
16 
15-E-0751, the Company proposed to use the results of the allocated 
17 
embedded cost of service study to set contract demand charges for SC10. 
18 
Q.    
What is the definition of marginal cost? 
19 
A. 
Since Central Hudson no longer owns significant electric generation or 
20 
any gas production facilities, marginal cost for the Company is defined as 
21 
the cost incurred to transmit and distribute an additional unit of electricity 
22 
or gas. 
23 
1613


--- Page 41 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE COST OF SERVICE PANEL 
26 
 
Q.    
Please discuss the preparation of marginal cost of service studies.  
1 
A. 
The Panel identified marginal transmission, distribution, and customer 
2 
costs.  Transmission and demand-related distribution costs so identified 
3 
were compiled into a marginal volumetric unit rate while the customer-
4 
related distribution and customer costs identified were compiled into a 
5 
marginal rate per customer. 
6 
Estimation of the marginal volumetric rate included identification of 
7 
the projected capital costs resulting from increased throughput due to 
8 
increased sales, including increased sales from existing customers as well 
9 
as increased sales from the connection of new customers.  We then 
10 
developed an economic carrying charge rate to be applied to the identified 
11 
capital costs to calculate the annual carrying cost on the plant investment.  
12 
Next, we estimated the marginal annual O&M expense associated with the 
13 
additional plant investment.  In the final step, we recognized the marginal 
14 
effect on working capital. 
15 
Estimation of the marginal customer cost included the calculation of 
16 
the economic carrying charge cost, marginal annual O&M expense, and 
17 
marginal effect on working capital of the customer portion of the projected 
18 
marginal capital costs identified during development of the marginal 
19 
volumetric rate.  The estimated marginal customer rate also includes other 
20 
customer related expenses not directly attributable to plant investment. 
21 
1614


--- Page 42 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE COST OF SERVICE PANEL 
27 
 
Q.    
Please explain how the projected capital costs resulting from increased 
1 
throughput due to increased sales were identified. 
2 
A. 
We analyzed the five year capital program forecast for calendar years 
3 
2024 through 2028 sponsored by the Company’s Electric, Gas, Common 
4 
and Technology Capital and Operations Panels to identify the projected 
5 
capital investment associated with increased throughput due to increased 
6 
sales.  As the Electric Capital and Operations Panel (“ECOP”) notes in its 
7 
testimony, the main driver of electric capital expenditures is not related to 
8 
load growth but to condition-based infrastructure replacement.  In fact, 
9 
93% of the planned expenditures in the “Production”, “Transmission”, 
10 
“Substation”, and “Distribution” categories are related to infrastructure 
11 
replacement.  The Company has experienced declining to stagnant 
12 
electric load growth and limited gas load growth, leading to a capital 
13 
program that is comprised predominantly of infrastructure projects.   As a 
14 
result, the Panel utilized a combination of the Company’s Transmission 
15 
Service Charge (“TSC”) and historic distribution investment in the 
16 
estimation of the electric marginal costs and utilized historic transmission 
17 
and distribution investment in the estimation of the gas marginal costs. 
18 
Q.    
Why did the Panel utilize historic data in the estimation of marginal costs? 
19 
A. 
The continued lack of electric load growth, and associated capital 
20 
expenditures as previously noted, required application of the same 
21 
methodology utilized for preparation of the electric MCOS studies 
22 
submitted in Cases 17-E-0459 and 20-E-0428.  This methodology was 
23 
1615


--- Page 43 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE COST OF SERVICE PANEL 
28 
 
also continued in preparing the gas MCOS study, applying the same 
1 
methodology utilized for preparation of the gas MCOS study submitted in 
2 
Case 20-G-0429 as new customer interest in natural gas service wanes 
3 
and use by existing customers declines.  Under this approach, inflation-
4 
adjusted actual cumulative distribution plant and gas transmission plant 
5 
expenditures were correlated to either normalized electric peak load/gas 
6 
deliveries or number of customers, depending on whether the plant was 
7 
identified as demand or customer related, with the resulting coefficient in 
8 
the equation utilized as the marginal estimate.  The TSC was used as a 
9 
proxy for electric transmission since Central Hudson is subject to the rules 
10 
of the New York Independent System Operator and the Company’s 
11 
transmission revenue requirement serves as the basis for the Company’s 
12 
TSC.  Entities, including Central Hudson, utilizing the Company’s 
13 
transmission system must pay the TSC, although no explicit payment is 
14 
made by the Company.  As a result, the TSC is the Company’s marginal 
15 
transmission cost.  Since a forecast of the TSC is not available, the 
16 
Company based the marginal transmission cost on a 12 month average of 
17 
the TSC adjusted by energy loss factors. 
18 
Q.    
Please explain how the economic carrying charge rate was developed. 
19 
A. 
A projection of the annual revenue requirements associated with the plant 
20 
investment was made for the life of the investment, including depreciation, 
21 
return (using the incremental cost of capital), income taxes, property taxes 
22 
and property insurance.  The present-worth of the stream of annual 
23 
1616


--- Page 44 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE COST OF SERVICE PANEL 
29 
 
revenue requirements was then adjusted to produce a levelized cost in 
1 
inflation-adjusted terms. 
2 
Q.    
Did you consider individual marginal O&M cost components? 
3 
A. 
Yes.  The Panel utilized an embedded methodology for O&M elements, 
4 
which, according to the National Association of Regulatory Utility 
5 
Commissioners, is a common and reasonable approach for these 
6 
components, rather than a strict marginal method.  We functionalized 
7 
historic O&M costs by function for 2018 through 2022 using the results 
8 
from the pro forma COS studies, and grouped them into demand-related 
9 
distribution and several customer-related categories. 
10 
Q.    
How was A&G expense addressed? 
11 
A. 
A&G expense was addressed through the development and application of 
12 
loaders, or adders, representing a historic average of inflation adjusted 
13 
A&G expense to plant and/or O&M. 
14 
Q.    
Were marginal cost estimates developed for all O&M and A&G expense 
15 
components? 
16 
A. 
No.  O&M and A&G recovered through the separate MFC Admin and MFC 
17 
Supply charges were excluded.  Costs recovered through separate 
18 
surcharges (including System Benefits Charge – reflective of the Clean 
19 
Energy Fund) were also excluded.  The Panel also excluded economic 
20 
development costs which we felt did not meet the definition of marginal for 
21 
study purposes. 
22 
1617


--- Page 45 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE COST OF SERVICE PANEL 
30 
 
Q.    
Please explain the Panel’s inclusion of a working capital component in the 
1 
marginal COS. 
2 
A. 
The working capital component reflects the revenue requirement for 
3 
materials and supplies inventory, certain prepayments and indirect costs 
4 
associated with a working capital cash allowance for O&M and A&G 
5 
expenses, all of which would be reflected in the Company’s rate base.  
6 
This is further discussed in the testimony of the Revenue Requirements 
7 
Panel. 
8 
Q.    
Has the Panel compared the results of the marginal COS studies to the 
9 
delivery rates developed in the embedded COS studies submitted in these 
10 
proceedings and to the delivery rates proposed by the FRP? 
11 
A. 
We have compared the results of the marginal COS studies to the 
12 
embedded COS studies but not to the delivery rates proposed by the FRP.  
13 
The Company followed this approach as the proposed delivery rates 
14 
developed by the FRP reflect reallocation of the revenue requirement 
15 
among service classifications based on the revenue allocation process 
16 
applied by the FRP.  The aforementioned comparison is presented on 
17 
Exhibit __ (COSP-6) and indicates that the marginal cost per kW/month is 
18 
lower for electric SC2 secondary and both SC 13 subclasses, while 
19 
electric SC 2 secondary and primary classes, SC 3, and both SC 13 
20 
subclasses show lower marginal customer rates.  The marginal customer 
21 
costs for gas are lower for the SC 11 subclasses and for SC 11 
22 
transmission volumetric costs. 
23 
1618


--- Page 46 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE COST OF SERVICE PANEL 
31 
 
Q. 
Why are the marginal rates generally higher than the embedded rates? 
1 
A. 
Generally, this is the function of higher per unit cost of new investment as 
2 
compared to lower embedded cost of existing investment, compounded by 
3 
weighted average service lives that approximate 60 years for electric plant 
4 
and 82 years for gas plant.  As a result, the average embedded cost tends 
5 
to be significantly lower.  
6 
Q. 
Has the Panel updated its analysis of gas marginal costs to serve electric 
7 
generators under SC 14–interruptible transportation service for electric 
8 
generation facilities? 
9 
A. 
No.  As a methodological review of such rates continues in Case 17-G-
10 
0011 (In the Matter of a Review of Tariff Provisions Regarding Natural 
11 
Gas Service to Electric Generators) and the two customers that the 
12 
Company serves under SC14 take service pursuant to the terms of 
13 
individually negotiated contracts, the Panel has not updated its analysis.   
14 
V. 
ELECTRIC LOSS FACTOR 
15 
Q.    
How are electric losses determined? 
16 
A. 
Electric losses are determined as the difference between the total 
17 
electricity received into the system and the total metered electricity 
18 
delivered to customers divided by the total electricity received.  The 
19 
electric loss factor is utilized to determine the factor of adjustment (“FOA”) 
20 
which is calculated as one divided by one minus the loss factor 
21 
percentage. 
22 
1619


--- Page 47 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE COST OF SERVICE PANEL 
32 
 
Q.    
What method is being used currently to determine the electric FOA for 
1 
Central Hudson? 
2 
A. 
Pursuant to the methodology established by the Commission in its June 
3 
18, 2010 Order in Case 09-E-0588, and continued by the Commission’s 
4 
June 17, 2015 Order in Case 14-E-0318, June 14, 2018 Order in Case 17-
5 
E-0459 and November 18, 2021 Order in Case 20-E-0428, the electric 
6 
system FOA is determined based on the electric loss factor calculated as 
7 
the most recent 36 month average based on the data available at the time 
8 
of compliance, allocated to service and/or sub-class based on the 
9 
methodology initially approved in Case 08-E-0887. 
10 
Q.    
Is the Company proposing to update the electric FOA currently in effect? 
11 
A. 
Yes.  The Company is proposing to update the electric FOA to reflect 
12 
more recent data.  Currently, the system factor of adjustment is 1.0112 
13 
based on the 36 month average ending September 2021.  The Company 
14 
proposes that the electric FOA be based on the electric loss factor 
15 
calculated as the most recent 36 month average based on the data 
16 
available at the time of compliance.  For comparison purposes only, the 
17 
system FOA based on the 36 months ended May 2023 is 1.0458, and the 
18 
resulting service class/sub-class FOAs are provided in the table below.  
19 
Data supporting the update of the electric system FOA, as well as the gas 
20 
system FOA, is provided in Exhibit __ (COSP-8). 
21

VI. 
GAS LOST AND UNACCOUNTED FOR 
3 
Q.    
What is gas lost and unaccounted for (“LAUF”)? 
4 
A. 
Gas LAUF is measured as the difference between the total gas received 
5 
into the system and the total metered gas delivered to customers divided 
6 
by the total gas received.  The LAUF is utilized to determine the FOA 
7 
which is calculated as one divided by one minus the LAUF percentage. 
8 
Q.    
What method is being used currently to determine the FOA for Central 
9 
Hudson? 
10 
A. 
Pursuant to the Commission’s June 17, 2015 Order in Case 14-G-0319, 
11 
and as modified by the Commission’s June 14, 2018 Order in Case 17-G-
12 
0460 and continued in the Commission’s November 18, 2021 Order in 
13 
Case 20-G-0429, the FOA is calculated as a five-year average for the 12 
14 
months ending August 31st of each year and updated annually to be 
15 
applicable for the period beginning with the first billing batch of November 
16 
and ending with the final billing batch of the subsequent October.  Annual 
17

4

IV. 
ALLOCATION OF DEFERRED INCOME TAXES 
4 
Q.    
Please describe the recommendation made by MI regarding the Panel’s 
5 
allocation of deferred income taxes. 
6 
A. 
MI Witness Pollock recommends, on page 12 of his testimony, that “all 
7 
non-current deferred income taxes and [Net Operating Losses (“NOL”)] 
8 
should be allocated to service classifications based on previously 
9 
allocated rate base” instead of allocating “these tax expenses on Rate 
10 
Year pre-tax operating income and taxable income.” 
11 
Q.    
What is MI Witness Pollock’s justification? 
12 
A. 
In his testimony, on page 12, MI Witness Pollock indicates that it is 
13 
unreasonable to allocate past period income tax expenses on Rate Year 
14 
pre-tax operating income and taxable income because “[p]ast period 
15 
income tax expenses are not caused by Rate Year pre-tax operating 
16 
income and taxable income.” 
17 
Q.    
Did MI Witness Pollock identify a certain aspect of deferred income taxes 
18 
he was concerned with? 
19 
A. 
Based on his workpapers supporting Exhibit __ (JP-6), in which MI 
20 
Witness Pollock reflects his recommended changes, including his changes 
21 
to the classification and allocation of prior period deferred income taxes 
22 
1632


--- Page 60 ---
Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419 
 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY OF THE COST OF SERVICE PANEL 
6 
 
and NOL, MI Witness Pollock’s concern focuses on the functionalization of 
1 
NOL, which in turn affects the allocation of deferred income taxes. 
2 
Q.    
Does the Panel functionalize and allocate all NOL and deferred income 
3 
taxes in the same manner? 
4 
A. 
No.  The Panel performs a detailed analysis to functionalize each 
5 
component of deferred income tax.  The resulting functionalized deferred 
6 
income taxes are then allocated to the classes in the same manner as the 
7 
underlying function.  For example, deferred income taxes functionalized 
8 
on total plant are spread to each production, transmission and distribution 
9 
function based on the proportion that each function comprises total plant.  
10 
In turn, total deferred income taxes – which includes the component 
11 
spread to the functions based on total plant as just described – are then 
12 
allocated to the classes using the same allocator employed to allocate the 
13 
underlying asset or expense. 
14 
Q.    
Please describe how the Panel functionalized NOL, including the Panel’s 
15 
justification for doing so. 
16 
A. 
The Panel functionalized NOL on pre-tax operating income and taxable 
17 
income.  The NOL carryforwards reflect the outcome(s) of previous tax 
18 
calculations that incorporate a diverse set of items and, as a result, the 
19 
NOL lacks the link to the specific items necessary for more granular 
20 
functionalization. 
21 
1633


--- Page 61 ---
Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419 
 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY OF THE COST OF SERVICE PANEL 
7 
 
Q.    
What are the implications on net deferred income taxes of MI’s proposal to 
1 
functionalize NOL on rate base rather than pre-tax operating income and 
2 
taxable income? 
3 
A. 
The Company analyzed MI’s proposal by reviewing MI Witness Pollock’s 
4 
workpapers supporting Exhibit __ (JP-6) and making corresponding 
5 
modifications to Exhibit __ (COSP-1) filed with this Panel’s direct 
6 
testimony.  Exhibit __ (COSP-6R) summarizes the Company’s analysis.  
7 
As shown in Schedule A of this exhibit, MI’s proposal increases the 
8 
functionalization of deferred income taxes to Distribution Line 
9 
Transformers, Distribution Services and Meters while reducing the 
10 
functionalization to all other functions.  As shown in Schedule B, the 
11 
resulting impact on class rates of return is a decrease in return for SC 1 
12 
non-heating, SC 2 non-demand and secondary demand, SC 13 substation 
13 
and transmission and SC 9. 
14 
Q.  
Does the Panel agree with MI Witness Pollock’s recommendation to 
15 
change the allocation of deferred income taxes? 
16 
A.  
No.  For the reasons stated above, the Panel does not agree with MI 
17 
Witness Pollock’s recommended treatment of deferred income taxes for 
18 
purposes of functionalization and allocation. 
19 
1634

Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419 
 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY OF THE COST OF SERVICE PANEL

V. 
PROPERTY TAXES 
1 
Q.    
Please describe the recommendation made by MI Witness Pollock 
2 
regarding the Panel’s functionalization of property taxes. 
3 
A. 
MI Witness Pollock recommends, on page 14 of his testimony, that 
4 
property tax expenses should be functionalized using net plant as 
5 
opposed to land and land rights investments, which were utilized by the 
6 
Company in its electric ECOS study. 
7 
Q.    
What is MI Witness Pollock’s justification? 
8 
A. 
MI Witness Pollock indicates on page 13 of his testimony that 
9 
“[f]unctionalizing property taxes on land and [land] rights investments 
10 
assigns a disproportionate amount of taxes to the production and 
11 
transmission functions and far too little to distribution and other functions.” 
12 
Q.  
Does the Panel agree with this recommendation? 
13 
A.  
Yes.  Although the Panel has been functionalizing property taxes on land 
14 
and land rights for at least the past three rate proceedings, the Panel 
15 
agrees it would be more appropriate to functionalize property taxes on net 
16 
plant, consistent with gas, as property taxes are assessed both on the 
17 
land as well as on the structures and/or equipment erected or affixed to 
18 
the land.  However, the Panel is concerned about the impact the resulting 
19 
change in methodology could have on the electric ECOS study results and 
20 
corresponding class rates of return. 
21 
1635


--- Page 63 ---
Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419 
 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY OF THE COST OF SERVICE PANEL 
9 
 
Q.    
Has the Panel reflected this change in the electric ECOS study presented 
1 
in Exhibit __ (COSP-1R)? 
2 
A. 
No. 
3 
Q.    
What would the impact be of the change in functionalization of property 
4 
taxes in the electric ECOS study? 
5 
A. 
Based on the Panel’s Exhibit __ (COSP-1) and corresponding 
6 
workpapers, which were utilized by MI Witness Pollock in his testimony, 
7 
changing the functionalization of property taxes to net plant would move 
8 
approximately $25 million or 56% of property taxes away from 
9 
transmission functions and towards distribution functions, including to 
10 
distribution lines and services, which currently do not contain property 
11 
taxes in the Company’s study.  A summary of the resulting change in 
12 
expense, including associated income tax changes, and return by class is 
13 
presented in Exhibit __ (COSP-7R).

Q.    
How does the Panel currently functionalize and allocate system control, 
1 
load dispatching, and other power supply expenses? 
2 
A. 
As MI Witness Pollock summarizes on page 9 of his testimony, the Panel 
3 
currently functionalizes these Federal Energy Regulatory Commission 
4 
(“FERC”) accounts 30% to Merchant Function Charge (“MFC”) Supply and 
5 
70% to the production function.  The associated operations and 
6 
maintenance (“O&M”) expense functionalized as production is then 
7 
allocated on energy. 
8 
Q.    
What is MI Witness Pollock’s justification to support the argument that the 
9 
Panel is functionalizing and allocating these items incorrectly? 
10 
A. 
On page 2 of MI Witness Pollock’s testimony, he states that functionalizing 
11 
system control, load dispatching, and other power supply expenses 
12 
entirely to production energy is “not consistent with cost causation.” 
13 
Q.  
Does the Panel agree with this recommendation? 
14 
A. 
Not entirely.  System control and load dispatching expense is not entirely 
15 
attributed to labor as MI Witness Pollock’s proposal assumes.  The Panel 
16 
maintains that it is appropriate to preserve consistency in the 
17 
functionalization of the plant asset and the functionalization of the 
18 
corresponding expense consistent with the Panel’s treatment of 
19 
transmission load dispatching expense as alluded to in MI Witness 
20 
Pollock’s testimony on page 10.  The Panel does acknowledge, however, 
21 
that there could be stronger linkage between the functionalization of 
22 
1637

production plant and production system control and load dispatch 
1 
expense.

3 
A. 
Should the Commission decide to direct a change in the allocation of 
4 
production system control and load dispatching expense, the Panel 
5 
suggests that modification should be made solely to the 70% 
6 
functionalized to production and should be based on the classification of 
7 
production plant, which is classified 92.5% to energy and 7.5% to demand.

Indeed, MI Witness Pollock found no issue with the Panel’s classification 
9 
of the underlying production plant assets.  This classification would follow 
10 
the Panel’s functionalization and classification of transmission load 
11 
dispatching expense which is functionalized and classified on total 
12 
transmission plant.

7

11

19

20 
well as production-related O&M costs are recovered through delivery 
21 
rates.  To the extent these hydro facilities are provided rate base 
22 
1640

Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419

treatment it is reasonable and appropriate to include these investments in

the ECOS studies.

1641

Q.  
Does the Panel agree with MI Witness Pollock’s interpretation of the 
1 
magnitude of market revenues? 
2 
A. 
No.  Neither the $18.6 million nor the $23.6 million were determined in a 
3 
manner that would be appropriate to impute in the ECOS study. 
4 
Q.    
Please describe the concerns the Panel has with the use of $18.6 million 
5 
(or $21.6 million as corrected) as the basis for determining an appropriate 
6 
level of estimated market revenues. 
7 
A. 
By imputing $18.6 million in revenue in the Rate Year ECOS study, MI 
8 
Witness Pollock failed to recognize that the sales revenues already 
9 
reflected in the ECOS study represent the pre-rate relief revenues 
10 
intended to collect the entire revenue requirement, including hydro 
11 
facilities, for which rate base treatment and base rate O&M recovery are

Q.

10

11

12 
Panel has continued to use 70 Heating Degree Days (“HDD”) to determine 
13 
the Gas Peak-Day sendout instead of the 73 HDD used to design gas 
14 
delivery facilities. 
15 
Q.  
Does the Panel agree with this recommendation? 
16 
A. 
Yes.  While the Panel updated the peak day degree days to 73 HDD in the 
17 
historic study it inadvertently failed to do so in the proforma Rate Year 
18 
study.  The Panel agrees that the peak degree days in the proforma Rate 
19 
Year study should be revised to reflect the design criteria of 73 HDD 
20 
rather than the 70 HDD included in the current calculation.

22

A. 
EAMs are metrics with targets and associated 
24 
1703

Cases 23-E-0418 and 23-E-0419

-6- 
 
earnings opportunities designed to align utility 
1 
shareholder interests with New York State energy 
2 
policy goals.  If the utility meets the EAM 
3 
targets, benefits are achieved, and utility 
4 
shareholders earn financial rewards.  The 
5

6 
19, 2016 Order Adopting a Ratemaking and Utility 
7 
Revenue Model Policy Framework in Case 14-M-
8 
0101, referred to as the Track Two Order.  
9 
Q. 
Provide a brief overview of the EAM requirements 
10 
set forth in the Track Two Order.  
11 
A. 
Pages 59 through 60 of the Track Two Order 
12 
explained the Commission’s expectation that EAMs 
13 
would play a transitional role until other forms 
14 
of market-based revenues are available.  The 
15 
Track Two Order established the Commission’s 
16 
preference for: outcome-based EAMs, on pages 61 
17 
through 65; EAMs that are generally positive-
18 
only, on pages 66 through 67; a maximum amount  
19 
of earnings totaling no more than 100 basis 
20 
points, on page 68; and establishing EAM awards 
21 
on a sliding scale with the size of awards 
22 
growing with greater achievement, on page 77. 
23 
The Track Two Order also set the Commission’s 
24 
1704

Cases 23-E-0418 and 23-E-0419

-7- 
 
expectations for the types of EAM metrics it 
1

2

4

Other Preliminary Recommendations 
17

18

19

20

21 
A. 
Yes.  We have one preliminary recommendation.  
22 
We recommend that the EAMs ultimately be 
23 
expressed in terms of absolute dollars instead 
24 
1717


--- Page 145 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 and 23-E-0419 
 
Staff EAMs Panel 
 
 
-20- 
 
of basis points.  While we express our potential 
1 
earnings in basis points in our testimony, we 
2 
recommend the values be converted to a dollar 
3 
amount once the capital structure and rate base 
4 
are determined in these proceedings.  
5 
Q. 
Explain why the Panel recommends that EAMs be 
6 
awarded in terms of dollars and not in basis 
7 
points.  
8 
A. 
Since the value of a basis point will fluctuate 
9 
over time, as it is directly related to the 
10 
amount of plant in service, basing EAMs strictly 
11 
on basis points could create a perverse 
12 
incentive for the Company to increase its net 
13 
plant in service to inflate the value of a basis 
14 
point.  Instead, the basis points assigned to 
15 
each EAM should be converted into absolute 
16 
dollar figures based on the equity ratio and net 
17 
plant in service targets ultimately approved by 
18 
the Commission in these proceedings. 
19 
Q. 
What are the dollar amounts associated with one 
20 
basis point for the Company’s electric and gas 
21 
businesses during the Rate Year?  
22

23 
revenue requirement, the value of one basis 
24 
1718

4

Electric and Gas Peak Reduction Metric EAMs 
1

2

4

12 
Q.

13

14 
A. 
The Gas Peak Reduction EAM is measured based on: 
15 
(a) the firm customer, who is a customer that 
16 
receives uninterruptible electricity and natural 
17 
gas supplies that are intended to be available 
18 
at all times during a period covered by an 
19 
agreement between the customer and utility; peak 
20 
demand on the winter peak demand day, in 
21 
dekatherms, or DTH; less the firm customer peak 
22 
demand on the maximum summer peak demand day, in 
23 
DTH - i.e., firm customer peak heating load in 
24 
1722


--- Page 150 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 and 23-E-0419 
 
Staff EAMs Panel 
 
 
-25- 
 
DTH; (b) divided by the number of Heating Degree 
1 
Days, or HDD, which is the measure of how cold 
2 
the temperature was on a winter peak demand day. 
3

Is the Company proposing any modifications to 
4 
its Gas Peak EAM?

No.

13 
this has historically had the greatest impact on 
14

system peak below a baseline level.  In the

system demand coincident with the annual NYISO

description of the Electric Peak Load Reduction

3 
Q. 
Why is the Panel recommending the 
4 
discontinuation of the Electric and Gas Peak 
5 
Load Reduction EAMs? 
6 
A. 
We have found that the attributes of the Gas and 
7 
Electric Peak Reduction metric can result in 
8 
financial gains, not due to any of the Company’s 
9 
actions, but rather because of unexpected 
10 
downturns in the economy or other negative 
11 
macroeconomic impacts.  Staff has observed this 
12 
with respect to several other utilities with 
13 
similar Electric or Gas Peak Reductions metrics, 
14 
specifically Con Edison, but also the KeySpan 
15 
Companies, NYSEG, and Orange & Rockland 
16 
Utilities, Inc. 
17 
Q. 
Please describe the experience with Con Edison. 
18 
A. 
In its testimony in Cases 22-E-0064 and 22-G-
19 
0065, on pages 45-50, the Staff Earnings 
20 
Adjustment Mechanisms Panel described how during 
21 
Calendar Years 2020 and 2021, Con Edison 
22 
significantly over-achieved its Electric Peak 
23 
Reduction EAM metric and nearly achieved the 
24 
1725


--- Page 153 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 and 23-E-0419 
 
Staff EAMs Panel 
 
 
-28- 
 
maximum target for its Gas Peak Reduction EAM 
1 
metric, primarily because of economic downturns 
2 
and changes in the ways that customers use 
3 
electricity and natural gas, likely because of 
4 
the COVID-19 pandemic. 
5 
Q. 
Have similar differences between observed and 
6 
forecasted values occurred in other services 
7 
territories? 
8 
A. 
Yes.  In the KeySpan NY and KeySpan LI rate 
9 
proceedings, Cases 23-G-0025 and 23-G-0026, 
10 
respectively, the Staff Efficiency Panel found 
11 
that the Gas Peak Reduction metrics established 
12 
for KeySpan NY and KeySpan LI were nearly 
13 
identical to those approved for Con Edison for 
14 
calendar years 2020 through 2022.  This 
15 
indicates that KeySpan NY and KeySpan LI 
16 
similarly realized financial gains from this EAM 
17 
because of certain unexpected macroeconomic 
18 
impacts, rather than because of the companies’ 
19 
own actions. 
20 
Q. 
Do Central Hudson’s EAM Reports indicate similar 
21

The Company files Annual Earning Adjustment

0460.  In the Earnings Adjustment Mechanisms

Q. 
Are there any actions that the Company could 
8

9 
A. 
It’s unlikely.  The Panel’s examination of EAM-
10 
related issues is illuminated by the lessons 
11 
learned from the electric and gas peak 
12 
reductions in other service territories.  
13 
Specifically, using the experience acquired from 
14 
the peak reduction metrics, the Panel strongly 
15 
recommends avoiding EAM metrics that could 
16 
potentially result in windfalls for utility 
17 
shareholders because of instances outside the 
18 
Company’s control, such as an economic downturn 
19 
or other negative macroeconomic stressors that 
20 
can affect how consumers use energy.

4

3 
Q. 
What is the policy reason for separating the 
4 
metrics? 
5 
A. 
When the Commission or the State has established 
6 
a target related to policy goals, such as the 
7 
CLCPA, but has not committed utility-specific 
8 
funding directly to their achievement, Staff 
9 
recommends that any EAM metrics designed to help 
10 
achieve such goals should have targets based 
11 
directly on achieving or exceeding them.  These 
12 
goals can be seen in the Order Expanding the NY-
13 
Sun Program, filed April 14, 2022 in Case 22-E-
14 
0629, in which the Commission established the 
15 
goal of achieving 10 gigawatts, or GW, of solar 
16 
production statewide by 2030, and in 
17 
Exhibit___(SEAMP-2), which records the 6 GW 
18 
energy storage goal announced by Governor Hochul 
19 
on December 28, 2022.  As these are two separate 
20 
policy goals, one for solar PV and one for BESS, 
21 
it is more appropriate to have two separate EAM 
22 
metrics to encourage the attainment of both 
23 
rather than combining them. 
24 
1732


--- Page 160 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 and 23-E-0419 
 
Staff EAMs Panel 
 
 
-35-

in splitting the DER Utilization EAM were used 
4 
in the most recent rate cases for Con Edison, 
5 
NYSEG, and RGE; Cases 22-E-0064, 22-E-0317, and 
6 
22-E-0319, respectively.  Each of these 
7 
variations employed adjustments best suited to 
8 
each utility’s unique service territory. 
9

10

11

12

16

18 
linear growth to achieve a reasonable 
19

20 
2,943 MW of incremental solar installations by

22 
approved NY-Sun Expansion Order.  
23 
Q.

for the Solar DER Utilization metric be

III. 
ELECTRIC AND GAS SALES FORECASTS 
1 
Q.    
Were the electric and gas forecasts for firm sales both developed in a 
2 
similar fashion and consistent with those presented by the Company in its 
3 
last three rate filings? 
4 
A. 
Yes. 
5 
Q.    
Would the Panel please provide an overview of the process by which the 
6 
forecast of electric own territory and firm gas sales were developed? 
7 
A. 
Customer forecasts were developed for each electric and gas customer 
8 
class.  For a number of these classes, sales volume forecasts were 
9 
developed on a sales per customer basis, with total sales specified as a 
10 
function of sales per customer and customer count.  Sales forecasts for 
11 
the remaining classes were developed on a total class basis. 
12 
Q.    
Why were forecasted sales volumes for certain classes developed on a 
13 
sales per customer basis? 
14 
A. 
Generally, this approach was applied to the classes with relatively large 
15 
numbers of customers.  Separating total consumption into customer and 
16 
sales per customer components recognizes that each component is 
17 
influenced by different factors and provides the opportunity to incorporate 
18 
more structure into the analysis of total consumption.  For instance, total 
19 
residential consumption can be influenced by such factors as customer 
20 
count (e.g., total number of residential customers), weather, and the 
21 
economy.  In this example, weather will most likely not influence the 
22 
1798


--- Page 226 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE FORECASTING AND RATES 
PANEL 
12 
 
number of customers, but could greatly influence use per customer.  As a 
1 
result, separating total consumption into components provides the 
2 
opportunity to incorporate more structure into the forecast of each 
3 
component.   
4 
Q.    
Were sales to full service customers (i.e., those customers continuing to 
5 
purchase their energy and/or natural gas requirements from Central 
6 
Hudson) addressed differently in the forecast than sales to retail access 
7 
and/or transport customers? 
8 
A. 
No.  In prior Central Hudson general rate proceedings (Cases 00-E-1273 
9 
and 00-G-1274), the Commission approved the unbundling of commodity 
10 
supply from delivery, resulting in the same base delivery rates for both full 
11 
service sales and retail access/transportation customers.  Therefore, the 
12 
sales forecasts presented reflect total full service and retail access 
13 
deliveries. 
14

and gas customer and sales levels?

the tables below.  The models developed to produce the forecasts were

estimated using actual monthly billed customer and sales data covering 
20 
the period January 2004 to December 2022.  As discussed later in our 
21 
testimony, estimation periods vary somewhat for the different classes in 
22

order to recognize structural changes, including changes to the billing

process and data quality issues that can sometimes limit data availability.

For example, revisions to billing cycles, in terms of customer composition, 
3 
and recording of customers’ end-use category (residential, commercial, 
4

Q.    
Please describe the types of variables utilized in the econometric models. 
5 
A. 
The econometric models make use of two types of variables: economic 
6 
and binary (or dummy).   
7 
1801


--- Page 229 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE FORECASTING AND RATES 
PANEL 
15 
 
Q.    
Would the Panel please explain what economic and binary variables are? 
1 
A. 
For the purposes of the forecasts presented here, economic variables 
2 
represent measurements of demographic or economic activity including, 
3 
but not limited to, such items as population, GDP, and household income.  
4 
Utilization of binary or “dummy” variables is reflected in many of the 
5 
customer and sales models presented here, consistent with standard 
6 
modeling practices.  In many instances, this type of variable was added as 
7 
a switch to turn various parameters on and off, such as differences in 
8 
odd/even month billing to reflect bimonthly billing for certain accounts 
9 
versus monthly billing, or to accommodate a specific data point to reduce 
10 
model error, while maintaining a longer estimation period. 
11 
Q.    
What is the source for the economic data utilized in the Company’s 
12 
forecast models? 
13 
A. 
Economic projections for the region served by the Company were based 
14 
on the August 2022 forecast provided by Moody’s Analytics.  Composite 
15 
forecast drivers for the Central Hudson region were constructed from the 
16 
four data regions included in the forecast that contain the Company’s 
17 
service territory:  Albany, Catskills, Dutchess County and Orange County.  
18 
The composite economic forecast drivers were calculated as a weighted 
19 
sum of the regional forecasts, where the weights reflect actual average 
20 
residential and non-residential sales in the region for calendar years 2020 
21 
1802


--- Page 230 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE FORECASTING AND RATES 
PANEL 
16 
 
through 2022.  These data were the latest available to the Panel at the 
1 
time of the preparation of our analyses.

Q.

3 
customer forecasts.

5 
residential classes, commercial classes, and Other Public Authority 
6 
(“OPA”) classes.  The small industrial demand and non-demand customer 
7 
forecasts were developed by maintaining the historic year levels.  As of

March 31, 2023, Central Hudson provided transmission or substation 
9 
service to twelve customers under the provisions of S.C. No. 13.  These 
10 
customers include customers who require service at transmission voltage 
11 
or who have provided all the necessary equipment to take service directly 
12 
from a substation.  The Company expects to stop providing service to two 
13 
of these customers prior to or during the course of the Rate Year bringing 
14 
the total number of S.C. No. 13 customers to ten.  The Company has 
15 
experienced diminishing customer growth in S.C. No. 5 (Area Lighting) 
16 
and little to no customer growth in S.C. No. 8 (Street Lighting) in recent 
17 
years which is discussed later in this testimony.  As a result, overall 
18 
contraction in area lighting customers is anticipated for the forecast period, 
19 
while the annual average street lighting customer level as of December 
20 
31, 2022 was maintained throughout the forecast period.  As approved by 
21 
the Commission in its Order issued October 25, 2001 in Case 00-E-1273, 
22 
1803

Q.

10

11

12 
holding customer counts at the most recent calendar year levels. 
13 
Q.    
What forecasting methods were used to project electric and gas sales 
14 
volumes? 
15 
A. 
Econometric models were constructed to forecast all electric classes, 
16 
excluding:  1) S.C. No. 13; 2) the three lighting classes; and 3) 
17 
interdepartmental.  As discussed later in our testimony, post-forecast 
18 
adjustments were then made to the electric sales forecast to account for 
19 
Electric Vehicles (“EV”) and photovoltaic (“PV”) net metering.  Post-
20 
forecast adjustments were also made to both the electric and gas sales 
21 
forecasts to account for heat pumps.  Econometric models were also 
22 
1804


--- Page 232 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE FORECASTING AND RATES 
PANEL 
18 
 
constructed for all firm gas classes, excluding interdepartmental and S.C. 
1 
No. 11.  Further, the forecasts developed for the electric residential and 
2 
commercial classes and all firm gas classes utilize Statistically Adjusted 
3 
End-Use (“SAE”) models. 
4 
Q.    
What is the SAE model approach? 
5 
A. 
The SAE approach integrates structural changes in end-use saturation 
6 
and efficiency trends, as well as addresses the interaction of economic 
7 
variables through the construction of end-use variables: heating, cooling 
8 
and other (base use).  These end-use variables include weather, price, 
9 
economic drivers and end-use saturation and efficiency trends.  
10 
Additionally, the electric end-use variables constructed for the residential 
11 
classes reflect changes in housing square footage and thermal shell 
12 
integrity. 
13 
Q.    
What is the source for end-use saturation and efficiency data? 
14 
A. 
Residential appliance and commercial end-use saturation and efficiency 
15 
trends are based on Energy Information Administration estimates for the 
16 
Middle Atlantic Census Region as compiled by Itron, Inc.  Where possible, 
17 
electric estimates are calibrated to Central Hudson’s service territory 
18 
based on results from the Company’s Residential Appliance Saturation 
19 
and/or Energy Management surveys. 
20 
Q.    
Would the Panel please describe these surveys? 
21 
1805


--- Page 233 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE FORECASTING AND RATES 
PANEL 
19 
 
A. 
Over the past forty-plus years, the Company surveyed its residential 
1 
customers thirteen times to obtain information about housing stock, 
2 
appliance saturation, usage patterns, preferences, and household 
3 
characteristics to initially assist in the determination of growth in energy 
4 
demand and later to assist in efforts to develop and promote effective 
5 
energy efficiency programs. 
6 
Q.    
Why does the Panel continue to support the SAE model approach? 
7 
A. 
Traditional regression models based solely on macro-economic variables 
8 
fail to fully explain the historic declining electric sales despite modest 
9 
economic growth over the past decade.  Customer and economic growth 
10 
has been more than offset by improvements in end-use efficiency driven 
11 
by changes in technology, standards, and programmatic efforts.  For 
12 
example, changes in lighting technology to LED fueled by changes in 
13 
standards, with adoption boosted by utility efficiency programs, have 
14 
resulted in significant declines in residential and commercial consumption.  
15 
The impact of these types of activities will be further intensified by 
16 
continued efforts to comply with New York State’s nation-leading clean 
17 
energy policies to reduce carbon emissions and address climate change.  
18 
The SAE model approach allows for the incorporation of end-use 
19 
intensities into traditional macro-economic regression models, providing 
20 
more structure, and explanatory power, as well as opportunities for 
21 
1806


--- Page 234 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE FORECASTING AND RATES 
PANEL 
20 
 
developing scenarios and accounting for specific actions, such as 
1 
programmatic efforts. 
2 
Q.    
Does the integration of end-use intensities with economics and weather 
3 
obscure variable transparency? 
4 
A. 
No.  The SAE approach recognizes the interaction of economic, weather, 
5 
and end-use intensities and provides a framework for constructing 
6 
integrated variables that address heating, cooling, and other end-uses.  
7 
The variables from macro-economic based regression analysis are still 
8 
utilized, but are combined with end-use intensities providing additional 
9 
model structure and an understanding of key energy drivers based on how 
10 
customers use electricity. 
11 
Q.    
Is the Panel aware of any other companies that utilize the SAE model 
12 
approach? 
13 
A. 
Yes.  In 2019, the New York Independent System Operator (“NYISO”) 
14 
completed the first phase of a multi-phase Climate Change Impact & 
15 
Resilience Study, which initially attempts to assess the potential 
16 
implications for load associated with climate change and the state’s 
17 
policies to promote beneficial electrification.1 Phase 1, completed under 
18 
contract by Itron, Inc., consisted of the development of forecasts for 
19 
energy and demand, evaluation of statewide temperature and humidity 
20 
trends, and forecast scenarios reflecting “the potential impacts of state 
21 
 
1  
Power Trends 2020, published by the New York Independent System Operator, p. 42. 
1807


--- Page 235 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE FORECASTING AND RATES 
PANEL 
21 
 
policies to address climate change, including the CLCPA [Climate 
1 
Leadership and Community Protection Act], accelerated adoption of 
2 
energy efficiency, and beneficial electrification.”2 The core finding of 
3 
Phase 1 “is that temperatures are rising across New York and will have a 
4 
significant impact on summer peak demand” however, “on an annual 
5 
basis, increasing temperatures have a minimal impact on system energy 
6 
requirements as increasing cooling sales are largely mitigated by 
7 
decreasing heating related sales.”3 Moreover, Phase 1 concluded that 
8 
“state policy designed to counter the impact of climate change may have 
9 
an even larger impact on load than increasing temperatures.”4 The SAE 
10 
model framework was integral to Itron’s forecast development and policy 
11 
assessment.

15

customers in each class.  Monthly forecast prices for each class include 
18 
applicable base delivery charges, and a projected delivery rate increase

effective July 1, 2024, as well as the Merchant Functions Charge (“MFC’),

Q.    
Please explain how the Panel selected the estimation period for each 
16 
model or individual forecast. 
17 
A. 
Generally, the Panel started with a prior version of each model.  After 
18 
updating the model to bring in actual monthly billing data through 
19 
December 2022, each model was re-specified, often in many iterations, 
20 
until a model was produced that the Panel considered to provide the most 
21 
reasonable results in terms of both statistical validation and production of 
22 
1809


--- Page 237 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE FORECASTING AND RATES 
PANEL 
23 
 
a realistic forecast.  This repeated re-specification of the model can 
1 
include different variables as well as changes to the estimation period.   
2 
Q.    
Is the utilization of different estimation periods between models or 
3 
forecasts a reasonable practice? 
4 
A. 
Yes.  Just as different models may utilize differing variables to explain 
5 
growth, different models may utilize differing estimation periods to address 
6 
structural changes resulting from economic, industry or business process 
7 
changes. 
8 
Q.    
Should the length of an estimation period correspond to the length of the 
9 
forecast period? 
10 
A. 
No.  The length of the estimation period should be sufficient to yield a 
11 
reasonable depiction of the identified future period, regardless of forecast 
12 
length, in order to inform decisions.  Utilization of a short estimation period 
13 
introduces the risk that the period of time selected does not sufficiently 
14 
embody activity for the item being forecast. 
15 
Q.    
How is weather incorporated into the sales models? 
16 
A. 
Monthly actual and normal heating degree days (“HDD”) and cooling 
17 
degree days (“CDD”) are transformed into billed HDDs to more closely 
18 
correspond to the sales billing periods.  For the majority of the classes, 
19 
billed degree days are incorporated in the heating, cooling and structural 
20 
variables, as described above, to reflect that energy use is not only 
21 
1810


--- Page 238 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE FORECASTING AND RATES 
PANEL 
24 
 
dependent on weather but also equipment saturation levels and operating 
1 
efficiencies. 
2 
Q.    
Please define a HDD. 
3 
A. 
Weather is expressed in terms of degree days measured over an electric 
4 
day and a gas day consistent with industry standard definitions of these 
5 
days and is based on hourly temperature readings obtained from the 
6 
Dutchess County Airport.  Electric HDDs are defined as the amount by 
7 
which 65 degrees Fahrenheit exceeds the average of the high and low 
8 
temperatures for a given day as measured midnight to midnight.  Gas 
9 
HDDs are defined as the amount by which 65 degrees Fahrenheit 
10 
exceeds the twenty-four hour average of temperatures for a given gas day 
11 
as measured 10 AM to 10 AM. 
12 
Q.    
Please define a CDD. 
13 
A. 
CDDs are measured for electric only and are defined as the amount by 
14 
which the average of the high and low temperatures for a given day, as 
15 
measured midnight to midnight, exceeds 65 degrees Fahrenheit. 
16 
Q.    
How does the Panel define normal weather for the purpose of forecasting 
17 
monthly billed sales? 
18 
A. 
Since 2008, and pursuant to the Commission’s Order issued on June 22, 
19 
2009 in Cases 08-E-0887 and 08-G-0888, the Company’s sales forecasts 
20 
have been developed based on normal weather conditions, where the 
21 
normal weather is determined by a ten-year average of monthly HDD or

PANEL

CDD, as applicable.  The forecasts presented by the Panel are based on a 
1

2

3 
Company prepared its sales forecast.  We recommend that the latest ten-
4

7

8 
A. 
Yes.  The Panel has continued to define the normal weather variable 
9 
utilized for the development of the annual gas capability forecast included 
10 
in the annual winter preparedness review as the 30-year average of HDD.  
11 
As required by Commission Order issued on June 17, 2015 in Case 14-G-
12

16 
Q.    
Why does the Panel believe that a ten-year weather variable provides a 
17 
more reasonable basis for sales forecasting purposes? 
18 
A. 
A continuing warming trend has been prevalent over the past several 
19 
decades.  In fact, the New York ISO Climate Change Impact Study issued 
20 
in December 2019 found that on average, the statewide average 
21 
temperature is increasing 0.7 degrees per decade since early 1990 and 
22 
1812


--- Page 240 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE FORECASTING AND RATES 
PANEL 
26 
 
the average temperature in Central Hudson’s service territory is increasing 
1 
at approximately 0.9 degrees per decade.  Therefore, utilization of a 30-
2 
year weather variable would be cold-biased, would not be representative 
3 
of expected weather, and would have significant implications on revenue 
4 
recovery as a result of higher sales forecasts, and resulting lower 
5 
volumetric rates.   
6 
Q.    
Why is a 30-year definition of normal weather appropriate for use in the 
7 
gas capability forecast? 
8 
A. 
The gas capability forecast seeks to determine the Company’s ability to 
9 
serve customers under extreme weather conditions in which case a cold-
10 
biased weather variable is appropriate.  As can be seen in the load 
11 
duration curve filed in Case 23-M-0230, the Company plans to meet load 
12 
under those extreme weather conditions for several days out of the entire 
13 
winter season – not for an entire month or winter season.   
14

IV. 
REVENUES 
1 
Q.    
How were the electric revenues associated with the sales forecast for 
2 
2023, 2024 and the Rate Year developed? 
3 
A. 
Monthly customer and sales forecasts for electric were developed on a 
4 
revenue account basis and allocated to a service class or sub-class based 
5 
on an average annual distribution of actual revenue account customers 
6 
and sales to service class or sub-class for the three years of 2020, 2021 
7 
and 2022.  Billing demands were projected based on historical load growth 
8 
factor trends.  The residential time-of-use (“TOU”) energy delivery split 
9 
between on-peak and off-peak periods were based on the actual annual 
10 
split realized for 2022.  The forecasted billing parameters derived were 
11 
priced at rates as filed by the Company in compliance with Case 20-E-
12 
0428. 
13 
Q.    
Do the forecasted revenues reflect recovery of any costs specifically 
14 
earmarked for certain customers?

1

4 
applied by subtracting $3.2 million from the profit as determined in step 
5 
one.  Ninety percent of the resulting shortfall or excess is collected from or 
6 
returned to customers.

7

8

9

13

15

16

17 
overall system average and those classes with a unitized rate of return 
18 
more than 115 percent of the system average received 0.75 times the 
19 
over system average.  The revenue allocation methodology is a three-step

process.

7

9

10

11 
eliminated as it is not aligned with the goals of the CLCPA and there is no 
12 
cost basis for providing such a differential to high volume retail customers.  
13 
In order to be mindful of bill impacts the Panel’s rate design proposes to 
14 
phase this change in over a two year period.

20

3 
G-0429 the first three years of a planned five year phase out of declining 
4

S.C. No. 2 or S.C. No. 13 Special Provision.  Given that this special 
2 
provision allows for these customers to be billed at the tail block rate of the 
3 
corresponding service class, and the Company anticipates that the tail 
4 
block discount will be eliminated effective July 2025 in favor of a flat 
5 
volumetric rate, the Company proposes to eliminate the Special Provisions 
6 
providing for these discounted rates at this time given lack of participation.   
7 
Q.    
Why is the Company moving to eliminate these rate differentials? 
8 
A. 
As further described in this Panel’s testimony in Case 20-G-0429, the 
9 
Company is making this change to improve the price signal of delivery rate 
10 
design. 
11 
Q.    
Is this change in rate structure aligned with any current Commission 
12 
objectives or policy goals? 
13 
A. 
Yes.  The elimination of declining block rates is consistent with the 
14 
Commission’s goal to promote energy efficiency.  Declining block rates 
15 
send the wrong price signal since prices decrease when consumption 
16 
increases.  Furthermore, in Case 20-G-0131 – Proceeding on Motion of 
17 
the Commission in Regard to Gas Planning Procedures, the Commission 
18 
identified rate design as a means to address peak reduction and 
19 
avoidance of infrastructure investment. 
20 
Q.    
Please elaborate on how the elimination of the rate block differential will 
21 
improve price signals. 
22 
1838


--- Page 266 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE FORECASTING AND RATES 
PANEL 
52 
 
A. 
The declining block rate structure was originally “intended to meet 
1 
competitive situations and promote the sale of gas by providing a lower 
2 
marginal cost of gas to larger customers”5.  Declining block rates reward 
3 
customers with a lower rate for greater usage, sending price signals that 
4 
do not align with the goals included within the CLCPA.  As shown by a 
5 
comparison of the marginal rate developed in Case 17-G-0460 to the 
6 
approved tail block rates in that case, the tail block rates do not send a 
7 
price signal reflective of the cost of each incremental unit of gas.  With the 
8 
underlying goal of promoting energy efficiency, declining block rates work 
9 
counter to that desired end result.  In particular, declining block rates limit 
10 
the ability of lower usage customers to achieve bill savings through energy 
11 
efficiency or conservation measures. 
12 
Q.    
Beyond improved price signals, what other benefits would be realized from 
13 
this proposed change? 
14 
A. 
The Company believes bill simplicity will be another benefit of the 
15 
proposed change.  Under flat rate design, customers will more easily be 
16 
able to ascertain how each incremental unit of gas impacts their total bill.   
17 
Q.    
How will this change affect customers? 
18 
A. 
The Company understands that the elimination of declining block rates will 
19 
result in some customers experiencing decreases while others experience 
20

72 
 
Q.    
Please describe the ECAM.   
1 
A. 
The ECAM consists of four components: the MPC, MPA, the 
2 
Miscellaneous Charges (“MISC”) and PPA. 
3 
Q.    
Please describe the MPC and MPA components of ECAM. 
4 
A. 
The MPC and MPA factors are applicable to all service classifications 
5 
excluding S.C. Nos. 2, 3 and 13 HPP as previously noted.  The MPC 
6 
charge recovers the Company’s cost of electricity supply related 
7 
purchases, including firm energy, capacity, ancillary charges, risk 
8 
management fees, and other charges imposed by the NYISO.  The MPC 
9 
also includes working capital carrying charges and an uncollectible 
10 
allowance.  Energy and capacity purchased under mandatory Independent 
11 
Power Producer (“IPP”) contracts and the Company’s retained generation 
12 
is priced at the monthly average of NYISO day-ahead market prices.  The 
13 
MPC charge is calculated on a monthly basis for each MPC group based 
14 
on actual costs incurred during the previous month allocated over 
15 
projected deliveries for the collection period.  The MPA is the 
16 
reconciliation mechanism for the MPC.  It is also calculated on a monthly 
17 
basis by MPC group and reconciles actual MPC recoveries with MPC 
18 
costs. 
19 
Q.    
Please describe any recent changes to the MPC billed to customers. 
20 
A. 
Effective April 1, 2017, the MPC is increased by a component designed to 
21 
recover from full service delivery customers the costs of Renewable 
22 
1859

Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____

PANEL 
73 
 
Energy Credits (“REC”), Zero Emissions Credits (“ZEC”) and Alternative 
1 
Compliance Payments (“ACP”) purchased pursuant to Case 15-E-0302.  
2 
Pursuant to Case 18-E-0071, the MPC shall also be increased to reflect 
3 
the costs of Offshore Wind Renewable Energy Credits (“OREC”).  
4 
Furthermore, effective January 1, 2023 the MPC is increased by a 
5 
component designed to recover from full service delivery customers the 
6 
costs related to the supply charge incentives paid to participants in the 
7 
Residential Managed Charging Program pursuant to the July 14, 2022 
8 
Order in Case 18-E-0138.

benefit or cost by estimated deliveries and is applicable to all energy

Q.    
Please describe the PPA component of ECAM. 
1 
A. 
The PPA factor is also applicable to all energy deliveries as service class 
2 
and sub-class specific PPA factors.  Prior to December 1, 2011, these 
3 
factors recovered the cost or benefit of the Company’s PPA with 
4 
Constellation Energy for energy and capacity from Nine Mile Point 2 
5

6 
Sharing Agreement (“RSA”) with Constellation.  Under the RSA, 
7 
Constellation was required to pay 80% of the net cumulative positive 
8 
spread, if any, between the actual revenues per MWh earned by NMP2 
9 
and the floor price per MWh for the period as set forth in the RSA.  The 
10 
RSA ran for ten years and expired in November 2021.  The Company is 
11 
proposing to remove the PPA component of ECAM from the tariff due to 
12 
the agreement’s expiration and the Company no longer collecting 
13 
revenues associated with the agreement. 
14

15

16

17 
available to S.C. Nos. 3 and 13 customers that continue to elect to 
18 
purchase their energy supply requirements from Central Hudson.  In Case 
19 
08-E-0887, the Company was required to expand HPP to all S.C. No. 2 
20 
customers exceeding 500 kW in any two months in a twelve month period.  
21 
In Case 09-E-0588, the Company was further required to expand HPP to

Q.

10

A.

12

13

14

15

17 
customers.  Despite the sale of the Company’s fossil and nuclear 
18

21

22

great majority of its customers.  The Company continues to source and

3

Q.    
How are the Company’s natural gas supply costs recovered from full 
7 
service customers? 
8 
A. 
Gas supply expense (demand and commodity) incurred by the Company 
9 
to serve full service customers taking service under S.C. Nos. 1 and 2 is 
10 
recovered through the GSC.  The GSC is determined monthly and 
11 
reconciled annually, for the twelve-month period ending August 31, in 
12 
accordance with 16 NYCRR §720-6.  The GSC is equal to the sum of the 
13 
average demand cost of gas and the average commodity cost of gas, 
14 
multiplied by the factor of adjustment and adjusted for the annual 
15 
reconciliation of gas expense, gas supplier refunds, interruptible sales 
16 
credits, capacity release credits, and all other adjustments as approved by 
17 
the Commission. 
18

20

21 
final Rate Year total revenue.
