Q.    
How is the RAM maximum recovery threshold currently determined and 
1 
how does the Company’s proposal differ from the current treatment? 
2 
A. 
Pursuant to the 2021 Rate Plan the RAM maximum recovery threshold is 
3 
established at 2.5% of total operating revenues.  This cap is calculated by 
4 
applying 2.5% to the Total Operating Revenues presented in the electric 
5 
and gas income statements adopted in the 2021 Rate Plan.  The 
6 
Company proposes to change this calculation to be based on 2.4% of total 
7 
revenue, inclusive of surcharges and commodity.  As described in the 
8 
Policy Panel and discussed further in the testimony of the Finance Panel, 
9 
current cash recovery of operating expenses is an important factor in 
10 
maintaining the Company’s financial integrity.

80 
 
A. 
The electric RDM is a revenue per class model applicable to S.C. Nos. 1, 
1 
2ND, 2PD, 2SD, 3, 5, 6, 8, 13 and 14.  Pursuant to the RDM, actual 
2 
delivery revenue by service class or sub-class for RDM eligible classes is 
3 
compared, on a monthly basis, to a delivery revenue target.  If the monthly 
4 
actual delivery revenue exceeds the delivery revenue target, the delivery 
5 
revenue excess is accrued for refund to customers at the end of the semi-
6 
annual RDM period (six months ending December or six months ending 
7 
June).  Likewise, if the monthly actual delivery revenue is less than the 
8 
delivery revenue target, the delivery revenue shortfall is accrued for 
9 
recovery from customers at the end of the semi-annual RDM period.  
10 
Effective July 1, 2021 the RDM was expanded to include S.C. No. 13 
11 
subject to certain deadbands and thresholds as detailed in Appendix O of 
12 
the Joint Proposal adopted by the Commission’s November 18, 2021 
13 
Order in Case 20-E-0428.6  At the end of a semi-annual RDM period, total 
14 
delivery revenue excess/shortfalls are refunded/surcharged to customers 
15 
through service class or sub-class specific RDM adjustments applicable 
16 
during a corresponding semi-RDM adjustment period (six months 
17 
beginning February 1 or six months beginning August 1 immediately 
18 
following the semi-annual RDM period).

5

6

7

8 
than a six month adjustment period this change in RDM adjustment 
9 
factors would have been a more gradual change from a credit to a 
10 
surcharge over time.  Elongating the reconciliation period would mitigate 
11 
bill impacts due to the seasonal nature of the gas business.

REBUTTAL TESTIMONY OF THE FORECASTING AND RATES 
PANEL 
8 
 
Q.    
What are SAE variables? 
1 
A. 
As noted in the Panel’s direct testimony, SAE variables are variables that 
2 
aid in addressing structural changes in end-use saturation and efficiency 
3 
trends.  These end-use variables include weather, price, economic drivers 
4 
and end-use saturation and efficiency trends.  Additionally, the electric 
5 
end-use variables constructed for the residential classes reflect changes 
6 
in housing square footage and thermal shell integrity. 
7 
Q.    
Does the SFP use an alternative to the SAE approach? 
8 
A. 
In their testimony, on page 20, the SFP states that they “separated out the 
9 
underlying components of these [SAE] variables and included them as 
10 
distinct explanatory variables on the right hand side of [their] models.” 
11 
However, in response to CH to DPS (061), the SFP clarified they did not 
12 
use any appliance saturation variables in their models and instead 
13 
included only economic, price or weather-related variables. 
14 
Q.    
Why does the Panel continue to support the use of SAE variables as the 
15 
preferred approach to forecasting sales? 
16 
A. 
The SAE approach of end use modeling is a widely recognized method of 
17 
forecasting energy sales.  As described in the Panel’s direct testimony, the 
18 
New York Independent System Operator (“NYISO”) leveraged the SAE 
19 
approach in 2019 when they completed the first phase of a multi-phase 
20 
Climate Change Impact & Resilience Study, which initially attempts to 
21 
assess the potential implications for load associated with climate change 
22 
1881


--- Page 309 ---
Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419 
 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY OF THE FORECASTING AND RATES 
PANEL 
9 
 
and the state’s policies to promote beneficial electrification.1  Phase 1, 
1 
completed under contract by Itron, Inc., consisted of the development of 
2 
forecasts for energy and demand, evaluation of statewide temperature 
3 
and humidity trends, and forecast scenarios reflecting “the potential 
4 
impacts of state policies to address climate change, including the CLCPA 
5 
[Climate Leadership and Community Protection Act], accelerated adoption 
6 
of energy efficiency, and beneficial electrification.”2  The core finding of 
7 
Phase 1 “is that temperatures are rising across New York and will have a 
8 
significant impact on summer peak demand” however, “on an annual 
9 
basis, increasing temperatures have a minimal impact on system energy 
10 
requirements as increasing cooling sales are largely mitigated by 
11 
decreasing heating related sales.”3  Moreover, Phase 1 concluded that 
12 
“state policy designed to counter the impact of climate change may have 
13 
an even larger impact on load than increasing temperatures.”4  The SAE 
14 
model framework was integral to Itron’s forecast development and policy 
15 
assessment.  Furthermore, the U.S. Energy Information Administration 
16 
(“EIA”), a nationally renowned source for trends and projections of energy 
17 
use and a source quote by the SFP themselves on page 14 of their 
18 
 
1  
NYISO, Power Trends 2020 (June 2020) at 42. 
2  
Id. 
3  
Itron Inc., on behalf of the NYISO, New York ISO Climate Change Impact Study, Phase 1: 
Long-Term Load Impact (December 2019) at 3. 
4  
Id.

testimony, utilizes the same type of end use modeling in the development

of its Annual Energy Outlook.

3

For the gas commercial non-heating customer and use per customer 
4

5 
relied upon linear regression to estimate the model while employing binary 
6

7

Q.    
Why does the Company express concern with that variable? 
10 
A. 
This variable name, which was provided in the Company’s workpapers, is 
11 
specific to non-manufacturing employment in the Catskill metro area, as 
12 
defined by Moody’s in their economic data sets.  The Company’s territory 
13 
expands through Albany, Dutchess, Orange and Catskill counties. 
14 
Developing a forecast driven by an economic variable specific to only one 
15 
portion of the Company’s territory may lead to inaccurate/skewed results. 
16 
It would be more appropriate for the SFP to use the economic variables 
17 
weighted by the Panel to the customers/sales in its entire territory.

Q.    
Please summarize MI Witness Pollock’s proposal to base class revenue 
1 
allocations solely on pro-forma ECOS studies. 
2 
A. 
On page 27 of his testimony, MI Witness Pollock proposes that electric 
3 
and gas class revenue allocations should be based solely on the results of 
4 
the pro-forma Rate Year ECOS studies. 
5 
Q.    
Please explain whether the Panel agrees with this proposal. 
6 
A. 
The Panel does not agree with MI Witness Pollock’s proposal to use only 
7 
the results of the pro-forma ECOS studies during revenue allocation.  
8 
Q.    
Why does the Company propose to leverage both the historic and pro 
9 
forma ECOS results in determining revenue allocation? 
10 
A. 
Central Hudson uses both the historic and pro-forma ECOS because 
11 
using only the result from the pro-forma has the potential to introduce a 
12 
layer of error because it reflects forecasted levels of revenue, expense, 
13 
and rate base along with forecasted customer and demand allocators.  If 
14 
the forecast of any of these items were subject to change, then the entire 
15 
results of the study would change.  This is important because if Central 
16 
Hudson were to rely solely on a pro-forma Cost of Service study it could 
17 
have impacts to each service classification as the Cost of Service results 
18 
are the basis for how Central Hudson allocates the rate increase to each 
19 
class.  In fact, even MI Witness Pollock acknowledges on page 27 of his 
20 
testimony that the studies may be unduly influenced by the circumstances 
21 
specific to each test year and the degree to which current delivery rates 
22 
1892


--- Page 320 ---
Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419 
 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY OF THE FORECASTING AND RATES 
PANEL 
20 
 
deviate from costs on a going-forward basis.  Even though the historic 
1 
ECOS study can be dated, it provides a basis for what actually happened 
2 
in a previous period and can be used as a tool to compare for 
3 
reasonableness.   
4 
Q.    
Is there any historical basis for the Company’s use of both historic and pro

forma ECOS results?

8 
those cases, the Company used the pro-forma ECOS and later adopted 
9 
Staff’s recommendation to use both the historic and pro forma in 
10 
determining the class increases.

7

8 
recovery from customers if the imputed revenue does not materialize, or, 
9 
in the absence of the continuation of the current Danskammer deferral, a 
10 
shortfall in the Company’s Rate Year revenue due to imputing an 
11 
uncertain level of revenues.  Should the SRP’s continuation of the 
12 
Danskammer imputation ultimately be adopted by the Commission, the 
13 
Panel proposes the imputation level be updated to $1.0 million which is 
14 
more reflective of the current Danskammer revenue level. 
15

16 
continuation of the existing Danskammer deferral? 
17 
A. 
The Panel does not agree that this deferral is not needed.  Neither the 
18 
SAP nor the SRP has provided any justification for the elimination of the 
19 
deferral.  As discussed in our direct testimony, Danskammer recently 
20 
reduced their firm supply (SC 11) contracted volume – which equates to a 
21 
reduction in SC 11 revenue – and uncertainty exists surrounding

Danskammer’s repowering plans.  Due to this uncertainty Danskammer’s

Perry points to the proper collection of demand-related costs through a

Q.    
Does the Panel agree with Walmart’s proposed rate design? 
5 
A. 
While the Panel agrees that full alignment between costs and rates is 
6 
ideal, other factors must be considered in rate design.  For example, as 
7 
Walmart Witness Perry identifies, a shift in recovery between demand and 
8 
energy delivery charges can have different impacts on customers of 
9 
different load factors.  Increasing cost recovery through the demand 
10 
charge would result in higher bill impacts to customers with lower load 
11 
factors.  Furthermore, energy delivery charges already comprise a 
12 
relatively small portion of the total delivery revenue requirement for these 
13 
classes currently representing 8.5% and 4.7% of SC 2 secondary and 
14 
primary demand revenue requirements, respectively.  In order to be 
15 
mindful of customer bill impacts for customers of all load factors, the Panel 
16 
maintains its original rate design approach to maintain the same relative 
17 
proportion of revenue collected through each component as is currently 
18 
recovered in rates.

Q.    
Does the Panel agree with UIU Witness Panko’s recommendation to 
1 
continue the gas tail block rate customer notification procedure? 
2 
A.

3 
testimony, which resulted from the Joint Proposal in Case 20-E-0248 and 
4 
20-G-0249, was to mail letters to customers that would experience a 
5 
delivery bill impact of 9% or more solely due to changes in the block 
6 
structure.  As described in the Company’s response to UIU-005, no 
7 
customer met the criteria to be sent a letter.  Furthermore, as shown in 
8 
Exhibit __ (FRP-16), the impact of the Company’s rate design proposal in 
9 
the Rate Year, prior to any rate change, would be a 3.7% total bill increase 
10 
for the highest users using 1,000 Ccf and a savings for all customers 
11 
using at or below the average residential usage.  A customers bill can 
12 
fluctuate in a given month by 3.7% or more solely due to changes in 
13 
usage, supply or other factors.  For this reason, the Panel does not agree 
14 
that a separate notification procedure should be established outside of the 
15 
existing gas rate design web page.

39 
 
Q.    
Please summarize the Staff Policy Panel (“SPP”)’s tariff changes related 
1 
to estimating and backbilling. 
2 
A. 
Staff’s tariff changes remove flexibility – through removing terms such as 
3 
‘ordinarily’ or ‘generally’ – that allow the Company to manage its meter 
4 
reading practices.  Staff’s tariff modifications also place restrictions on the 
5 
number of adjusted bills that may be issued in a billing period or 
6 
consecutive estimated bills that may be issued to residential customers. 
7 
Q.    
Does the Panel have any concerns with this language? 
8 
A. 
Yes.  The practice of issuing an estimated or adjusted bill is contemplated 
9 
and allowed under the New York State Public Service Law.  As discussed 
10 
further in the rebuttal testimony of the Customer Experience Panel, there 
11 
exist other abnormal operating conditions outside the Company’s control 
12 
that may necessitate an off-cycle estimate.  For example, the SPP’s tariff 
13 
modifications identify “staffing failures” as a reason explicitly excluded 
14 
from circumstances qualifying as exceptions for purposes of reading 
15 
meters. This definition is overly broad and as further discussed in the 
16 
rebuttal testimony of the CEP, storms or other abnormal operating 
17 
conditions are likely to require meter readers to perform other roles. The 
18 
rebuttal testimony of the Company’s CEP also addresses Staff’s proposal 
19 
related to the tariff amendments to provide a $20 customer bill credit if the 
20 
Company renders an estimated bill. 
21 
1912

12

13

III. 
STAFF GAS FORECAST CORRECTIONS 
1 
Q.    
Please summarize the gas forecast modifications presented in the Staff 
2 
UFPP testimony. 
3 
A. 
As stated on page 1 of their testimony, the SUFPP corrected two gas 
4 
model errors made in the Staff Forecasting Panel’s (“SFP”) initial prepared 
5 
testimony.  Specifically, as described on pages 2 – 3 of its testimony, the 
6 
SUFPP explains that it: 1) updated the price variable used in three of the 
7 
panel’s  gas models which previously omitted rate increases prior to 2009; 
8 
and 2) corrected the inadvertent use of the Staff panel’s calculated 
9 
residential price of gas instead of the commercial price of gas in its 
10 
commercial heat use-per-customer model. 
11 
Q.    
Does the Panel agree these corrections are appropriate? 
12 
A. 
Yes. 
13 
Q.    
Did Staff correct any of the issues with the Staff gas models addressed by 
14 
this Panel’s rebuttal testimony? 
15 
A. 
No.  As seen in Exhibit __ (SUFPP-3) at pages 10 – 11, Staff continued to 
16 
use the economic variables “FENMPNYP06,” which is the variable for 
17 
private non-manufacturing employment for Catskill.  As described in our 
18 
rebuttal testimony, it is not appropriate to use an economic variable 
19 
representative of a specific portion of the Company’s service territory 
20 
when forecasting customers and sales representative of the Company’s 
21 
entire service territory. 
22 
1921

IV. 
ELECTRIC REVENUE PRICE OUT CORRECTIONS 
2 
Q.    
Please describe the corrections Staff made to the electric revenue price 
3 
out. 
4 
A. 
On pages 9 – 10 of their testimony, the SUFPP states that they made a 
5 
minor correction to include a $20,000 revenue decrease associated with 
6 
energy efficiency inadvertently left out of the Staff Rate Panel’s (“SRP”) 
7 
initial prepared testimony exhibits and associated revenue price out as 
8 
well as a correction to remove a double count of SC 6 Alternative Time-of-
9 
Use revenues equating to approximately $625,000. 
10 
Q.    
Please describe the SUFPP’s energy efficiency adjustment of $20,000.  
11 
A. 
As described in this Panel’s direct testimony, customers participating in 
12 
the Recharge New York Program are exempt from paying for energy 
13 
efficiency programs included in base rates and receive an EE Tracker 
14 
credit to credit them for such amounts.  The SUFPP’s adjustment for 
15 
$20,916 represents the EE Tracker credit for applicable customers in 
16 
Service Classification Nos. 2 and 3.  
17 
Q.    
Does the Panel agree that this correction needs to be made? 
18 
A. 
Yes.  This amount was reflected in our direct testimony exhibits and 
19 
associated revenue price out. 
20 
.  
21

3

4

V. 
POST-MODEL ADJUSTMENTS 
6 
Q.    
Please summarize the post-model adjustments performed by this Panel. 
7 
A. 
As described further in our direct testimony, we performed post-model 
8 
adjustments to account for Electric Vehicles (“EV”) and PV net metering in 
9 
the electric sales forecast and to account for heat pumps in both the 
10 
electric and gas sales forecasts. 
11 
Q.    
Did the SUFPP perform the same types of post-model adjustments as the 
12 
Panel? 
13 
A. 
While the SUFPP approached the calculations in a different manner, their 
14 
updated testimony and exhibits reflect these post-model adjustments, 
15 
which were previously omitted from the SFP and SRP initial prepared 
16 
testimony exhibits and associated revenue price out. 
17 
Q.    
Does Staff’s PV post-model adjustment differ from the Company’s? 
18 
A. 
Yes.  There are several differences between the Company’s and Staff’s 
19 
adjustment, including: 1) Staff did not add back the lost sales attributed to 
20 
net metering prior to forecasting use per customer as the Company did; 2) 
21 
Staff’s workpapers appear to include a linking error which leverages the 
22 
1923


--- Page 351 ---
Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419 
 
SUPPLEMENTAL REBUTTAL TESTIMONY OF THE 
FORECASTING AND RATES PANEL 
5 
 
prior year installed kW rather than the current year installed kW for 
1 
purposes of calculating lost kWh; and 3) Staff’s calculations do not 
2 
account for installed kW in prior months when determining the lost kWh for 
3 
the current month and instead only reflects lost kWh associated with PV 
4 
installations in that month. 
5 
Q.    
Please describe how the Panel performed its PV post-model adjustment. 
6 
A. 
In our workpapers titled “ResSales_2024.NDM” and 
7 
“ComSales_2024.NDM”, we added back an estimate of lost kWh each 
8 
month beginning with the first month of installed net metered kW for the 
9 
residential non-heating, commercial non-demand, and commercial 
10 
demand classes.  These lost kWh were added back to actual billed kW 
11 
resulting in a data set grossed up for net metering that was used as the 
12 
basis for developing forecasted use-per-customer for these classes.  
13 
Then, a post-forecast adjustment was made to reduce the resulting 
14 
forecast by the cumulative effect of forecasted net metered installations. 
15 
Q.    
Did the SUFPP utilize the same add back method? 
16 
A. 
No.  Instead of utilizing the add back method, Staff used only the 
17 
incremental component of the Company’s PV forecast starting in the 
18 
month of October 2023 – or the first month for which the SUFPP 
19 
performed a forecast. 
20 
1924


--- Page 352 ---
Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419 
 
SUPPLEMENTAL REBUTTAL TESTIMONY OF THE 
FORECASTING AND RATES PANEL 
6 
 
Q.    
Does the Panel have any concerns with Staff’s approach? 
1 
A. 
Yes.  In addition to a minor linking issue in which Staff’s workpaper 
2 
appears to link to the wrong row for purposes of determining cumulative 
3 
installed kW, the SUFPP’s approach fails to recognize the cumulative 
4 
effect of PV installed in a prior month. 
5 
Q.    
Please describe how the SUFPP reflected installed kW of PV. 
6 
A. 
The SUFPP leveraged the Company’s cumulative monthly installed PV 
7 
forecast but for purposes of determining their adjustment they quantified 
8 
the applicable installed kW as the difference between current month and 
9 
prior month installed kW. 
10 
Q.    
What are the Panel’s concerns with this approach? 
11 
A. 
While this is different than the Company’s approach to add back the 
12 
historic realized lost kWh, the Panel recognizes that this is a valid 
13 
alternative approach, assuming the SUFPP did not add back the realized 
14 
lost kWh to the underlying sales data used in the use-per-customer 
15 
models. 
16 
Staff’s method, however, focused solely on incremental PV installed after 
17 
September 2023 – the last data point of actual billed sales data utilized by 
18 
the SUFPP – it failed to recognize the cumulative effect of PV installed 
19 
between October 2023 through the end of the Rate Year.  
20 
1925


--- Page 353 ---
Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419 
 
SUPPLEMENTAL REBUTTAL TESTIMONY OF THE 
FORECASTING AND RATES PANEL 
7 
 
Q.    
Please elaborate. 
1 
A. 
As shown in Exhibit __ (FRP-1SR), the cumulative kW that contributes to 
2 
lost kWh sales due to net metering in a month should be reflective of not 
3 
only PV systems installed in the current month but also PV systems 
4 
installed in prior months.  For example, Schedule A of Exhibit __ (FRP-
5 
1SR) shows that the forecasted 1,486 kW of PV installed in October 2023 
6 
should not only affect the month of October 2023 but should also be taken 
7 
into account in each subsequent month during the forecast period as 
8 
these systems will continue to generate kWh each month.  In Schedule B, 
9 
we have corrected Staff’s installed kW in column D, using a base period of 
10 
September 2023 as Staff did, but instead reflecting the cumulative 
11 
installed kW since September based on our calculations shown in 
12 
Schedule A.  The resulting cumulative installed kW was multiplied by 
13 
1,189 kWh and based on the monthly distribution of generation, as used 
14 
by both Staff and the Company, to result in a corrected kWh adjustment 
15 
as shown in column F of Schedule B. 
16 
Q.    
Has the Panel quantified the impact of this correction? 
17 
A. 
Yes.  Instead of the $94,740 reduction to revenues as proposed by the 
18 
SUFPP, if Staff’s underlying methodology to estimate the effect of net 
19 
metering on the electric sales forecast is adopted, the associated 
20 
adjustment should be a reduction of $1,470,000. 
21 
1926


--- Page 354 ---
Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419 
 
SUPPLEMENTAL REBUTTAL TESTIMONY OF THE 
FORECASTING AND RATES PANEL 
8 
 
Q.    
Moving on to the topic of the EV post-model adjustment, please 
1 
summarize how this Panel calculated the adjustment. 
2 
A. 
Utilizing a forecast of cumulative EVs in the Company’s service territory 
3 
over the period 2021 – 2028, we projected the number of annual 
4 
incremental EVs compared to a base period of year-end 2022.  The 
5 
resulting incremental EVs were converted to kWh based on the 
6 
assumptions shown in Exhibit __ (FRP-9) Schedule A. 
7 
Q.    
How does Staff’s EV post-model adjustment differ?  
8 
A. 
While leveraging the same underlying forecast, Staff calculated a trend 
9 
reflective of EV growth embedded in historic actuals that included only the 
10 
incremental EV growth above that trend in their post-model adjustment. 
11 
Q.    
Is the Panel concerned with  Staff’s approach? 
12 
A. 
Yes.  While the Panel recognizes Staff’s approach to reflect only the 
13 
incremental EVs registered after September 2023, we question whether a 
14 
trend based on two actual data points (2021 and 2022) and a prorated 
15 
portion of the forecast 2023 data point is sufficient to establish a slope 
16 
above which incremental EVs are measured.  Furthermore, EVs 
17 
purchased in a year may not contribute to the full estimated annual 
18 
incremental kWh until the following year depending on the timing of 
19 
vehicle purchase.  For this reason, and since 2021 was the first year for 
20 
which the Company obtained EV registration data, the Panel’s approach 
21 
that utilized year-end 2022 as the basis from which to determine 
22 
1927

Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419

FORECASTING AND RATES PANEL 
9 
 
incremental EVs for the purpose of the EV post-model kWh adjustment, 
1 
should be adopted. 
2 
Q. 
Does this conclude your testimony at this time? 
3 
A. 
Yes, it does.
